<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We classify finite Euclidean Ramsey configurations by a necessary and sufficient tensor condition over their coordinate fields. The Ramsey property here concerns monochromatic congruent copies at the original scale under arbitrary finite colorings. The criterion shows that every nonempty subtransitive set and every nonempty set of at most five points on a circle is Ramsey. In particular, some Ramsey cyclic quadrilaterals are not subtransitive, disproving the necessity direction of the Leader–Russell–Walters conjectured characterization.

- **claim-tensor-classification** (classification, [source](#stmt-thm-classification)): Let $A=\{a_1,\ldots,a_s\}\subset\mathbb R^d$ be a finite set of at least two points with affine span $\mathbb R^d$, and define $p_i,F,B,m_F$ as above. Then $A$ is Ramsey if and only if there is a matrix $P\in\operatorname{Mat}_{d+1}(B)$ such that $$\begin{aligned} (p_i\otimes1)^T P(1\otimes p_i)&=0 &&(1\le i\le s),\label{eq:field-evaluations}\\ m_F(P_{\alpha\beta})&=\delta_{\alpha\beta} &&(1\le\alpha,\beta\le d).\label{eq:field-spatial} \end{aligned}$$ Here the entries of $p_i\otimes1$ and $1\otimes p_i$ are formed componentwise. There are no separate conditions on the multiplied constant row or constant column of $P$.
  - Explanation: A finite configuration spanning its ambient space is Ramsey exactly when a matrix over the tensor square of its coordinate field kills every augmented point on both sides while its multiplied spatial block is the identity.
- **claim-matrix-tensor-equivalence** (theorem, [source](#stmt-alg-equivalence)): The matrix condition in Theorem <a href="#thm:classification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:classification">1</a> is equivalent to <a href="#alg:tensor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alg:tensor">[alg:tensor]</a>.
  - Explanation: The matrix condition over the coordinate tensor ring is equivalent to the existence of a flip-invariant rational tensor on affine functions with vanishing evaluations and identity gradient Gram matrix.
- **claim-necessity-coloring** (theorem, [source](#stmt-alg-necessity)): If <a href="#alg:tensor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alg:tensor">[alg:tensor]</a> has no solution, then there is an integer $r_0\geq2$ such that every $\mathbb R^D$ admits an $r_0$-coloring with no monochromatic congruent copy of $A$. In particular, every Euclidean Ramsey set satisfies the matrix condition of Theorem <a href="#thm:classification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:classification">1</a>.
  - Explanation: If the tensor system has no solution, algebraic separation yields a fixed finite coloring of every dimension with no monochromatic congruent copy, so Ramsey sets satisfy the matrix condition.
- **claim-finite-lattice-identity** (construction, [source](#stmt-stencil-finite)): Let $k,s\geq1$, let $\Lambda_1,\ldots,\Lambda_s$ be subgroups of $\Gamma=\mathbb Z^k$, and put $V_i=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb Q}\Lambda_i$. Let $C\in\operatorname{Mat}_k(\mathbb Q)$ be symmetric. Suppose that the image of $\sum_{a,b}C_{ab}\varepsilon_a\otimes\varepsilon_b$ in $(\mathbb Q^k/V_i)\otimes_{\mathbb Q}(\mathbb Q^k/V_i)$ is zero for every $i$, where $\varepsilon_1,\ldots,\varepsilon_k$ is the standard basis of $\mathbb Q^k$. There is a finitely supported function $\nu:\Gamma\to\mathbb Q$ such that $$\label{stencil:cosets} \sum_{\gamma\in q}\nu(\gamma)=0 \qquad(q\in\Gamma/\Lambda_i,\ 1\leq i\leq s)$$ and $$\label{stencil:moments} \sum_\gamma\nu(\gamma)=0,\qquad \sum_\gamma\nu(\gamma)\gamma=0,\qquad \sum_\gamma\nu(\gamma)\gamma\gamma^{\mathsf T}=C.$$
  - Explanation: Vanishing tensor images on the quotient lattices produce a finitely supported rational weight function whose coset sums vanish and whose second moment is the given symmetric matrix.
- **claim-two-scale-configurations** (construction, [source](#stmt-pairs-two-scale)): Let $A=\{a_1,\ldots,a_s\}\subset\mathbb R^d$ have affine span $\mathbb R^d$, with $s\geq2$. Let $W=\mathbb R^{1+d}$ be regarded as a vector space over $\mathbb Q$. Suppose that a symmetric tensor $T\in W\otimes_{\mathbb Q}W$ satisfies $$(e_i\otimes e_i)(T)=0\quad(1\leq i\leq s), \qquad G(T)=I_d,$$ where $e_i(v,u)=v+a_i\cdot u$. For every $\eta>0$, there are an integer $\ell\geq1$ and vectors $f_i,g_i\in\mathbb R^\ell$ such that $$\begin{aligned} \\|f_i-f_j\\|^2&=\\|a_i-a_j\\|^2, &\\|g_i-g_j\\|^2&=q\\|a_i-a_j\\|^2, &q&=\frac{\eta}{1+\eta}, \label{pairs:distances} \end{aligned}$$ for every $i,j$, and $g_i$ is a permutation of the coordinates of $f_i$ for each $i$.
  - Explanation: A tensor certificate yields two embeddings of the configuration at different scales whose corresponding points have the same coordinate values up to permutation, with the scale ratio arbitrarily small.
- **claim-smoothing-bump** (theorem, [source](#stmt-pairs-bump)): Let $C$ be a real symmetric $k\times k$ matrix and let $U\in\operatorname{Mat}_{d\times k}(\mathbb R)$, where $d\geq1$ and $UCU^{\mathsf T}=I_d$. For every $\eta>0$, there is a nonnegative $b\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^k)$ such that $$\label{pairs:bump-bound} \int_{\mathbb R^k}b(x)\,dx>0, \qquad \int_{\mathbb R^k}(D_Cb)_-(x)\\|Ux\\|^2\,dx <\eta\int_{\mathbb R^k}b(x)\,dx.$$
  - Explanation: Averaging Gaussian densities and truncating gives a compactly supported smooth nonnegative function for which the negative part of the second-order operator, weighted by the gradient norm, is arbitrarily small relative to its mass.
- **claim-finite-rational-weights** (construction, [source](#stmt-pairs-rational)): Let $\Gamma=\mathbb Z^k$, and let $\Lambda_1,\ldots,\Lambda_s$ be subgroups of $\Gamma$. Suppose that a finitely supported function $\nu:\Gamma\to\mathbb Q$ has zero pushforward on each $\Gamma/\Lambda_i$, and satisfies $$\sum_\gamma\nu(\gamma)=0, \qquad \sum_\gamma\nu(\gamma)\gamma=0, \qquad \sum_\gamma\nu(\gamma)\gamma\gamma^{\mathsf T}=C.$$ Let $U$ be a real $d\times k$ matrix with $UCU^{\mathsf T}=I_d$, where $d\geq1$. For every $\eta>0$, there are an integer $n\geq1$, a finitely supported $\mu:\Gamma\to\mathbb Q$, and $\zeta\in\mathbb Q_{>0}$ such that $\mu$ has zero pushforward on each $\Gamma/\Lambda_i$ and $$\begin{aligned} \sum_\lambda\mu(\lambda) \frac{\lambda}{n}\frac{\lambda^{\mathsf T}}{n} &=\zeta C, \label{pairs:exact-moment}\\ \sum_\lambda\mu(\lambda)_- \left\\|U\frac{\lambda}{n}\right\\|^2 &<\eta\zeta. \label{pairs:small-negative} \end{aligned}$$
  - Explanation: Convolving the lattice weights with a discretised smoothing function and perturbing to rational values keeps exact cancellation on every coset while giving the exact second moment and a small negative gradient moment.
- **claim-path-criterion** (theorem, [source](#stmt-paths-criterion)): Suppose that for every integer $n\ge1$ there are two paths with the same endpoint and weights $\lambda>0$ and $\mu\ge0$ satisfying $\mu/\lambda<1/n$. Then $A$ is Euclidean Ramsey.
  - Explanation: Equal-endpoint paths with arbitrarily small weight ratios force the Ramsey property, via Hales–Jewett lines, ultrafilter products realised by strings, and compactness.
- **claim-compactness-reduction** (theorem, [source](#stmt-paths-compactness)): Fix an integer $r\ge2$. If every $r$-coloring of $\mathcal E$ contains a monochromatic congruent copy of $A$, then the same holds for every $r$-coloring of $\mathbb R^D$, for some finite $D$.
  - Explanation: Monochromatic copies for every coloring of the finitely supported sequence space already occur in some finite-dimensional Euclidean space, by compactness of a product of finite sets.
- **claim-weight-synchronization** (theorem, [source](#stmt-paths-synchronization)): Fix an integer $n\ge1$. Let two paths have the same endpoint and weights $\lambda>0$ and $\mu\ge0$. If $\rho=\mu/\lambda<1/n$, then there is an endpoint $w\in K^s$ admitting a path of weight $1/t$ for every integer $1\le t\le n$.
  - Explanation: Two paths with a common endpoint and small weight ratio can be rescaled and concatenated over an interval partition to give one endpoint reachable by paths of every reciprocal weight up to the fixed bound.
- **claim-ultrafilter-corner-group** (theorem, [source](#stmt-paths-corner-group)): The operation in <a href="#paths:ultrafilter-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="paths:ultrafilter-product">[paths:ultrafilter-product]</a> makes $\beta\mathcal S$ a compact right-topological semigroup with identity $\delta_\epsilon$. It contains an idempotent $p$ such that $p(\beta\mathcal S)p$ is a group with identity $p$.
  - Explanation: The ultrafilter space on the string monoid is a compact right-topological semigroup containing an idempotent whose corner is a group, obtained from minimal ideals.
- **claim-simultaneous-realization** (theorem, [source](#stmt-paths-realization)): Let $q_{i,j}\in\beta\mathcal S$ for $1\le i\le s$ and $1\le j\le M$, where $M\ge1$. Suppose that every column $j$ has one of the following specified forms:* 1. *$q_{i,j}=q_j$ is independent of $i$;* 2. *$q_{i,j}=\delta_{z_{i,j}}$ for prescribed strings $z_{i,j}\in\mathcal S$.* *If $B\in q_{i,1}\cdots q_{i,M}$ for every $i$, there are strings $x_{i,j}$ such that all products $x_{i,1}\cdots x_{i,M}$ belong to $B$, with $x_{i,j}$ independent of $i$ in each column of the first form and $x_{i,j}=z_{i,j}$ in each column of the second form.
  - Explanation: A set containing an ultrafilter product in every role contains a product of actual strings, chosen left to right so that common columns use identical strings and principal columns use the prescribed ones.
- **claim-pair-projections-group** (theorem, [source](#stmt-groups-pairs)): For every $i\ne j$, the monoid $M_{ij}$ is a group containing $N\times N$.
  - Explanation: Projecting the copy monoid to any two coordinates gives a group that contains the product of the degree-zero kernel with itself.
- **claim-right-fractions** (theorem, [source](#stmt-groups-fractions)): Each $L_i$ is a normal subgroup of $K$, and its cost satisfies $$c_i(g^{-1})=c_i(g),\qquad c_i(kgk^{-1})=c_i(g),\qquad c_i(gh)\le c_i(g)+c_i(h)$$ for $g,h\in L_i$ and $k\in K$. Every tuple $(g_i)_{i=1}^s$ with $g_i\in L_i$ has a represented right fraction of cost at most $\sum_i c_i(g_i)$. Moreover, $$\label{groups:derived-inclusion} N^{(s-2)}\subset L_i\qquad(1\le i\le s),$$ where $N^{(0)}=N$ and $N^{(r+1)}=[N^{(r)},N^{(r)}]$.
  - Explanation: Single-coordinate right fractions form normal subgroups with a cost that is inversion- and conjugation-invariant and subadditive, and iterated commutator subgroups of the kernel lie inside them.
- **claim-orbit-averaging** (theorem, [source](#stmt-groups-averaging)): Let $H$ be a finite group of coordinate permutations of $\mathcal E$, and let $Z\subset\mathcal E$ be a finite $H$-invariant set. For every $\delta>0$, there is a tuple $w\in K^Z$ represented by diagonal factors and factors $(\xi_{h x})_{x\in Z}$, $h\in H$, with degree $m>0$, for which $$\label{groups:orbit-bound} w_xw_y^{-1}\in L,\qquad c(w_xw_y^{-1})\le\delta m \quad\text{whenever }x,y\text{ belong to the same }H\text{-orbit}.$$
  - Explanation: Repeated averaging over a finite permutation group makes within-orbit differences lie in the correction subgroup with cost arbitrarily small relative to the degree.
- **claim-equal-endpoints** (theorem, [source](#stmt-groups-endpoints)): Suppose $q>0$ and $f_i,g_i\in\mathbb R^\ell$ satisfy $$\\|f_i-f_j\\|^2=\\|a_i-a_j\\|^2,\qquad \\|g_i-g_j\\|^2=q\\|a_i-a_j\\|^2 \quad(1\le i,j\le s),$$ and each $g_i$ is a coordinate permutation of $f_i$. For every $\delta>0$ there exist two paths with the same endpoint and weights $\lambda>0$ and $\mu\ge0$ such that $$\frac{\mu}{\lambda}\le q+s\delta.$$
  - Explanation: Two-scale coordinate-permutation pairs give two paths with equal endpoints whose weight ratio is at most the scale ratio plus a small multiple of the tolerance.
- **claim-ramsey-implies-spherical** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cons-spherical)): Every finite Euclidean Ramsey set is spherical.
  - Explanation: Multiplying the evaluation equations in the tensor criterion yields an ordinary sphere through all points, so every finite Ramsey set is spherical.
- **claim-subtransitive-ramsey** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cons-transitive)): Every nonempty subset of a finite transitive Euclidean set is Ramsey.
  - Explanation: Averaging coordinate rows over the isometry group of a finite transitive set builds a symmetric rational tensor certificate, so all its nonempty subsets are Ramsey.
- **claim-quadratic-independence** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cons-quadratic)): Let $A=\{a_1,\ldots,a_s\}\subset\mathbb R^d$ be spherical and affinely span $\mathbb R^d$. Put $p_i=(1,a_i)^{\mathsf T}$ and let $F$ be its coordinate field. If the $s$ row vectors $$(p_{i\alpha}p_{i\beta})_{0\le\alpha,\beta\le d} \quad(1\le i\le s)$$ are linearly independent over $F$, then $A$ is Ramsey.
  - Explanation: For a spherical spanning set, linear independence of the quadratic evaluation rows over the coordinate field lets an adjugate argument lift a field solution to a solution over the tensor ring.
- **claim-five-circle-points** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cons-quadrilateral)): Every nonempty set of at most five distinct points on a circle is Ramsey. In particular, every cyclic quadrilateral is Ramsey.
  - Explanation: Products of two line equations give independent quadratic evaluations on five circle points, so any set of at most five circle points, in particular any cyclic quadrilateral, is Ramsey.
- **claim-transcendental-kites** (counterexample, [source](#stmt-cons-kites)): For every transcendental $a\in(-1,1)$, the cyclic quadrilateral $$K_a=\bigl\{(-1,0),(1,0), (a,\sqrt{1-a^2}),(a,-\sqrt{1-a^2})\bigr\}$$ is Ramsey and is not subtransitive.
  - Explanation: Cyclic kites with a transcendental parameter are Ramsey yet not subtransitive, disproving the necessity direction of the subtransitive characterization.
- **claim-nine-generic-points** (counterexample, [source](#stmt-cons-nine)): Let $t_1,\ldots,t_9$ be algebraically independent real numbers over $\mathbb Q$. The nine points $$a_i=\left(\frac{1-t_i^2}{1+t_i^2}, \frac{2t_i}{1+t_i^2}\right),\qquad 1\le i\le9,$$ form a spherical set that is not Ramsey.
  - Explanation: Nine circle points from algebraically independent parameters form a spherical set whose tensor evaluation matrix has nonzero determinant, so the criterion fails and the set is not Ramsey.
- **claim-twelve-squares** (counterexample, [source](#stmt-cons-twelve)): Set $$t=\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}10^{-m!},\qquad u=\frac{1-t^2}{1+t^2},\qquad v=\frac{2t}{1+t^2}, \qquad Q_0=\{(\pm1,0),(0,\pm1)\},$$ and define $$R_+=\begin{pmatrix}u&-v\\v&u\end{pmatrix},\qquad R_-=\begin{pmatrix}u&v\\-v&u\end{pmatrix},\qquad X=R_+Q_0\cup R_-Q_0\cup Q_0.$$ Then $X$ is a twelve-point subset of the unit circle that is not Ramsey.
  - Explanation: Three rotated coordinate squares with a Liouville-type parameter admit a derivation-weighted moment identity forcing a contradiction, so the twelve points are not Ramsey.

      },

        "exact_quote": "Let $k,s\\geq1$, let $\\Lambda_1,\\ldots,\\Lambda_s$ be subgroups of $\\Gamma=\\mathbb Z^k$, and put $V_i=\\operatorname{span}_{\\mathbb Q}\\Lambda_i$. Let $C\\in\\operatorname{Mat}_k(\\mathbb Q)$ be symmetric. Suppose that the image of $\\sum_{a,b}C_{ab}\\varepsilon_a\\otimes\\varepsilon_b$ in $(\\mathbb Q^k/V_i)\\otimes_{\\mathbb Q}(\\mathbb Q^k/V_i)$ is zero for every $i$, where $\\varepsilon_1,\\ldots,\\varepsilon_k$ is the standard basis of $\\mathbb Q^k$. There is a finitely supported function $\\nu:\\Gamma\\to\\mathbb Q$ such that $$\\label{stencil:cosets}\n \\sum_{\\gamma\\in q}\\nu(\\gamma)=0\n \\qquad(q\\in\\Gamma/\\Lambda_i,\\ 1\\leq i\\leq s)$$ and $$\\label{stencil:moments}\n \\sum_\\gamma\\nu(\\gamma)=0,\\qquad\n \\sum_\\gamma\\nu(\\gamma)\\gamma=0,\\qquad\n \\sum_\\gamma\\nu(\\gamma)\\gamma\\gamma^{\\mathsf T}=C.$$",

      },

        "anchor": "stmt-pairs-two-scale",
        "exact_quote": "Let $A=\\{a_1,\\ldots,a_s\\}\\subset\\mathbb R^d$ have affine span $\\mathbb R^d$, with $s\\geq2$. Let $W=\\mathbb R^{1+d}$ be regarded as a vector space over $\\mathbb Q$. Suppose that a symmetric tensor $T\\in W\\otimes_{\\mathbb Q}W$ satisfies $$(e_i\\otimes e_i)(T)=0\\quad(1\\leq i\\leq s),\n \\qquad G(T)=I_d,$$ where $e_i(v,u)=v+a_i\\cdot u$. For every $\\eta>0$, there are an integer $\\ell\\geq1$ and vectors $f_i,g_i\\in\\mathbb R^\\ell$ such that $$\\begin{aligned}\n \\|f_i-f_j\\|^2&=\\|a_i-a_j\\|^2,\n &\\|g_i-g_j\\|^2&=q\\|a_i-a_j\\|^2,\n &q&=\\frac{\\eta}{1+\\eta}, \\label{pairs:distances}\n\\end{aligned}$$ for every $i,j$, and $g_i$ is a permutation of the coordinates of $f_i$ for each $i$.",
        "source_id": "PM-172.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-pairs-two-scale",

        "anchor": "stmt-pairs-rational",
        "exact_quote": "Let $\\Gamma=\\mathbb Z^k$, and let $\\Lambda_1,\\ldots,\\Lambda_s$ be subgroups of $\\Gamma$. Suppose that a finitely supported function $\\nu:\\Gamma\\to\\mathbb Q$ has zero pushforward on each $\\Gamma/\\Lambda_i$, and satisfies $$\\sum_\\gamma\\nu(\\gamma)=0,\n \\qquad \\sum_\\gamma\\nu(\\gamma)\\gamma=0,\n \\qquad \\sum_\\gamma\\nu(\\gamma)\\gamma\\gamma^{\\mathsf T}=C.$$ Let $U$ be a real $d\\times k$ matrix with $UCU^{\\mathsf T}=I_d$, where $d\\geq1$. For every $\\eta>0$, there are an integer $n\\geq1$, a finitely supported $\\mu:\\Gamma\\to\\mathbb Q$, and $\\zeta\\in\\mathbb Q_{>0}$ such that $\\mu$ has zero pushforward on each $\\Gamma/\\Lambda_i$ and $$\\begin{aligned}\n \\sum_\\lambda\\mu(\\lambda)\n       \\frac{\\lambda}{n}\\frac{\\lambda^{\\mathsf T}}{n}\n   &=\\zeta C, \\label{pairs:exact-moment}\\\\\n \\sum_\\lambda\\mu(\\lambda)_-\n       \\left\\|U\\frac{\\lambda}{n}\\right\\|^2\n   &<\\eta\\zeta. \\label{pairs:small-negative}\n\\end{aligned}$$",
        "source_id": "PM-172.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-pairs-rational",
          "exact_quote": "Let $\\Gamma=\\mathbb Z^k$, and let $\\Lambda_1,\\ldots,\\Lambda_s$ be subgroups of $\\Gamma$. For every $\\eta>0$, there are an integer $n\\geq1$, a finitely supported $\\mu:\\Gamma\\to\\mathbb Q$, and $\\zeta\\in\\mathbb Q_{>0}$ such that $\\mu$ has zero pushforward on each $\\Gamma/\\Lambda_i$ and $$\\begin{aligned}\n \\sum_\\lambda\\mu(\\lambda)\n       \\frac{\\lambda}{n}\\frac{\\lambda^{\\mathsf T}}{n}\n   &=\\zeta C, \\label{pairs:exact-moment}\\\\\n \\sum_\\lambda\\mu(\\lambda)_-\n       \\left\\|U\\frac{\\lambda}{n}\\right\\|^2\n   &<\\eta\\zeta. \\label{pairs:small-negative}\n\\end{aligned}$$",
          "source_id": "PM-172.1"

      "conclusion": {

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Ramsey set** ([source](#pm-h-2)): A finite nonempty set $A$ in a Euclidean space is *Ramsey* if, for every integer $r\ge2$, there is an integer $D\ge1$ such that every map $c:\mathbb R^D\to\{1,\ldots,r\}$ is constant on a congruent copy of $A$.
- **Congruence** ([source](#pm-h-2)): Congruence means preservation of every pairwise distance, at the original scale.
- **Transitive set** ([source](#pm-h-2)): Transitivity means that the isometries of the finite set act transitively on its points.
- **Subtransitive set** ([source](#pm-h-2)): An isometric subset of such a set is called *subtransitive*.
- **Coordinate field F and tensor ring B** ([source](#pm-h-3)): Define $$p_i=\begin{pmatrix}1\\a_i\end{pmatrix},\qquad F=\mathbb Q\bigl((a_i)_\alpha:1\le i\le s,\ 1\le\alpha\le d\bigr), \qquad B=F\otimes_{\mathbb Q}F.$$
- **Multiplication homomorphism m_F** ([source](#pm-h-3)): The commutative ring $B$ has a multiplication homomorphism $$m_F:B\longrightarrow F,\qquad m_F(x\otimes y)=xy.$$
- **Evaluation map** ([source](#alg:tensor)): For each label define the $\mathbb Q$-linear evaluation map $$e_i:W\longrightarrow\mathbb R,\qquad e_i(v,u)=v+a_i\cdot u.$$
- **Symmetric tensor space** ([source](#alg:tensor)): Let $\mathcal T(W)$ be the subspace of $W\otimes_{\mathbb Q}W$ fixed by the flip $w\otimes w'\mapsto w'\otimes w$.
- **Evaluation lattices** ([source](#stencil:section)): Set $\Gamma=\mathbb Z^k$ and define the evaluation lattices $$\Lambda_i=\{\lambda\in\Gamma:e_i(w(\lambda))=0\}, \qquad V_i=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb Q}\Lambda_i.$$
- **Path** ([source](#paths:section)): A *path* is a chosen finite sequence of factors in $K^s$ of the following two kinds:
- **Endpoint and weight** ([source](#paths:section)): The *endpoint* is the product of the factors in $K^s$, in their given order. The *weight* is the sum of their assigned weights.
- **Diagonal element notation** ([source](#paths:section)): Write $\Delta g=(g,\ldots,g)\in K^s$ for $g\in K$.
- **Ultrafilter on strings** ([source](#pm-h-11)): An ultrafilter on $\mathcal S$ is a maximal proper filter of subsets of $\mathcal S$.
- **Copy monoid** ([source](#pm-h-14)): Let $M\subset K^s$ be the monoid generated by the diagonal subgroup $\Delta K$ and the tuples $X_b=(\xi_{b_i})_{i=1}^s$ associated to ordered congruent copies $b$ of $A$.
- **Right fraction and cost** ([source](#pm-h-14)): We call $uv^{-1}$ a *right fraction*. Its cost, with chosen expressions for $u,v$, is the sum of their degrees.

| q-final-scale | scale ratio chosen in the sufficiency proof | < | $1/(2n)$ | Fixed positive integer n |
| q-final-tolerance | tolerance chosen in the sufficiency proof | = | $1/(2sn)$ | Fixed positive integer n and s points |

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **s-equivalence** [claim](#stmt-alg-equivalence) / [proof](#proof-stmt-alg-equivalence): Symmetrise over the flip; descend from the real tensor square to the field tensor square by a basis expansion and row reduction. -> symmetric rational tensor condition
- **s-necessity** [claim](#stmt-alg-necessity) / [proof](#proof-stmt-alg-necessity): Separate the target from the image by a rational linear functional, then colour by interval labels of reduced values. -> finite coloring avoiding the set in every dimension
- **s-lattice** [claim](#stmt-stencil-finite) / [proof](#proof-stmt-stencil-finite): Laurent group algebra, logarithmic coordinates, flatness of completion, truncation to a finite polynomial. -> rational weight function with coset and moment identities
- **s-bump** [claim](#stmt-pairs-bump) / [proof](#proof-stmt-pairs-bump): Average Gaussian densities between two covariances, then cut off smoothly. -> smoothing function with small negative part
- **s-rational** [claim](#stmt-pairs-rational) / [proof](#proof-stmt-pairs-rational): Convolve with a discretised smoothing function, use Riemann sums, then perturb to rational data. -> rational weights with exact moment and small negative part
- **s-two-scale** [claim](#stmt-pairs-two-scale) / [proof](#proof-stmt-pairs-rational-2): Split weights into positive and negative lists, add a common Gram increment, rescale. -> two embeddings at different scales with permuted coordinates
- **s-compactness** [claim](#stmt-paths-compactness) / [proof](#proof-stmt-paths-compactness): Compactness of a product of finite colour sets. -> finite dimension for each number of colors
- **s-synchronization** [claim](#stmt-paths-synchronization) / [proof](#proof-stmt-paths-synchronization): Partition the unit interval, rescale both paths on each piece, concatenate in fixed order. -> one endpoint with paths of every reciprocal weight
- **s-corner-group** [claim](#stmt-paths-corner-group) / [proof](#proof-stmt-paths-corner-group): Minimal compact left ideal, minimal subsemigroup, idempotent, left inverses in the corner. -> idempotent with group corner
- **s-realization** [claim](#stmt-paths-realization) / [proof](#proof-stmt-paths-realization): Choose strings column by column while maintaining a prefix invariant. -> strings inside the given set with common factors aligned
- **s-path-criterion** [claim](#stmt-paths-criterion) / [proof](#proof-stmt-paths-realization-2): Hales–Jewett line on a colouring defined through the corner group, with expanded paths at variable positions. -> monochromatic congruent copy in the sequence space and then in finite dimension
- **s-pair-groups** [claim](#stmt-groups-pairs) / [proof](#proof-stmt-groups-pairs): Any two points at the right distance extend to a copy; connectivity of the distance graph generates the kernel. -> pair projections are groups containing the kernel square
- **s-fractions** [claim](#stmt-groups-fractions) / [proof](#proof-stmt-groups-fractions): Manipulate fractions for inversion, conjugation and products; induct on coordinates for commutator subgroups. -> normal subgroups with subadditive cost
- **s-averaging** [claim](#stmt-groups-averaging) / [proof](#proof-stmt-groups-averaging): Averaging products raise derived depth; repeated products contract relative cost. -> tuple with small relative within-orbit cost
- **s-endpoints** [claim](#stmt-groups-endpoints) / [proof](#proof-stmt-groups-endpoints): Apply averaging to the two orbits, correct endpoint discrepancy by right fractions. -> two equal-endpoint paths with controlled weight ratio
- **s-sufficiency** [claim](#stmt-thm-classification) / [proof](#proof-stmt-groups-endpoints-2): Combine the equivalence, two-scale pairs, equal endpoints and the path criterion. -> Ramsey property
- **s-spherical** [claim](#stmt-cons-spherical) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cons-spherical): Apply the multiplication map to the evaluation equations. -> sphere through all points
- **s-transitive** [claim](#stmt-cons-transitive) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cons-transitive): Average affine coordinate rows over the isometry group to build a tensor certificate. -> tensor condition for all subsets
- **s-quadratic** [claim](#stmt-cons-quadratic) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cons-quadratic): Solve over the field, then lift with an adjugate identity over the tensor ring. -> criterion matrix
- **s-circle-five** [claim](#stmt-cons-quadrilateral) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cons-quadrilateral): Products of two line equations give independent quadratic evaluations. -> Ramsey property
- **s-kites** [claim](#stmt-cons-kites) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cons-kites): Apply the five-point result and cite the known non-embedding. -> Ramsey and not subtransitive
- **s-nine** [claim](#stmt-cons-nine) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cons-nine): Show the evaluation determinant is nonzero by specialisation, hence only the zero matrix solves the system. -> failure of the criterion
- **s-twelve** [claim](#stmt-cons-twelve) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cons-twelve): Weighted derivation moments on the coordinate field contradict the identity block. -> failure of the criterion

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-quadratic-row-independence**: For a spherical configuration that affinely spans its ambient space, linear independence over the coordinate field of the quadratic evaluation rows at the points is a sufficient certificate that the configuration is Ramsey.
- **inv-circle-five-points**: Small sets of distinct points on a circle always satisfy the row-independence certificate, hence are Ramsey.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-nine-circle-points**: Assuming that any larger set of points on a circle is Ramsey because sphericity holds and small circle sets are Ramsey. -> Generic points on a circle with algebraically independent parameters fail the tensor condition, so the set is not Ramsey even though it is spherical.
- **anti-numeric-coordinates**: Running the certificate on floating-point approximations of the coordinates. -> The criterion depends on exact relations in the coordinate field, which approximate values cannot recover.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `algorithmic`. The row-independence hypothesis of the quadratic-interpolation proposition is a finite exact linear-algebra check; the harness computes this rank over the rationals for points of a circle given by rational parameters, which is the finite computation behind the five-point circle corollary. It does not decide the Ramsey property in general.

```python
# INPUTS: one line of rational parameters t_i (tokens like 1/2 or -1) separated by spaces
# PRECONDITIONS: parameters give distinct points of the unit circle via the rational parametrization; coordinates rational
# OUTPUTS: one integer, the rank over the rationals of the matrix with rows (p_a p_b) for p=(1,x,y)
# TIME: polynomial in the number of points (Gaussian elimination on a matrix with nine columns)
# SPACE: linear in the number of points
# ARITHMETIC: exact Fraction arithmetic, no floating point
# FAILURE: malformed token raises an exception and a nonzero exit code
import sys
from fractions import Fraction

def pts(ts):
    out = []
    for t in ts:
        x=(1-t*t)/(1+t*t)
        y=2*t/(1+t*t)
        out.append((x, y))
    return out

def rank(rows):
    rows = [r[:] for r in rows]
    rk = 0
    ncol = len(rows[0])
    for c in range(ncol):
        piv = None
        for r in range(rk, len(rows)):
            if rows[r][c] != 0:
                piv = r
                break
        if piv is None:
            continue
        rows[rk], rows[piv] = rows[piv], rows[rk]
        for r in range(rk + 1, len(rows)):
            f = rows[r][c] / rows[rk][c]
            rows[r] = [x - f * y for x, y in zip(rows[r], rows[rk])]
        rk += 1
    return rk

def main():
    toks = sys.stdin.read().split()
    ts = [Fraction(k) for k in toks]
    rows = []
    for (x, y) in pts(ts):
        p = [Fraction(1), x, y]
        rows.append([p[a] * p[b] for a in range(3) for b in range(3)])
    print(rank(rows))

main()
```

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper has no program or library. Its criterion is a statement about matrices over a tensor ring of exact coordinate fields. It does not specify any data layout, interface or procedure a program could implement as written. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Nothing in the paper concerns agents, rules, physics or procedural content. Colorings are arbitrary functions with no regularity, so they cannot be simulated or sampled as a model. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No checker or utility is specified. A tool could only test a tensor certificate when exact field relations are supplied, and the paper gives no such certificate procedure. |
| algorithms | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The main theorem is an existence criterion, not a procedure. The sufficiency proof uses compactness and ultrafilter arguments that give no computable construction or running time. The rank test in the quadratic proposition is only a sufficient condition and is stated without any algorithm or complexity. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper defines no data structure. Its free groups, monoids of paths and ultrafilter semigroups are proof devices on infinite objects and have no finite representation with operations or costs. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | There is no numerical kernel or fixed-size arithmetic pipeline in the paper. The criterion relies on exact equality in a tensor product of fields, which is not a workload to offload. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper has no hardness assumption, adversary or protocol. Nothing in its statements bears on keys, hashes or secure computation. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No learning problem, data distribution or loss appears. Colorings in the paper are adversarial, arbitrary functions, not trained models, so no generalisation statement follows. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | There is no objective, constraint set or optimum. The compactness step yields only some finite dimension for each number of colors, with no quantitative bound that could size a resource. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper has no processes, messages, faults or consensus. Its colorings are global functions on Euclidean space, with no notion of locality or communication. |
| other | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The nearest use is as a geometric classification: classifying which finite point configurations are forced to appear monochromatically. It is a theory result, not an engineering construction. Sets of at most five circle points are Ramsey, while nine generic circle points and a particular twelve-point set are not. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-exact-coordinates** (hypothesis): The criterion works with exact relations in the coordinate field. It is not a procedure for recovering those relations from approximate real coordinates, so it cannot be run on rounded input. Blocked use: ['Deciding Ramsey membership from floating-point coordinates', 'Tolerance-based geometry checks that treat near-equal values as equal']
- **limit-spanning-two-points** (hypothesis): The classification is stated for a finite set of at least two points whose affine span is the whole ambient space. A singleton is handled separately, and the criterion is read on a spanning congruent representative. Blocked use: ['Applying the matrix condition to a set that does not span the ambient space without first passing to its affine span', 'Applying the matrix condition to a single point']
- **limit-multiplied-vs-tensor-equalities** (formal_scope): Equalities in the tensor ring B are stronger than their images under the multiplication map. Sphericity only gives the multiplied equations, so it does not imply the tensor condition. Blocked use: ['Concluding that a spherical set is Ramsey from the ordinary sphere equation alone', 'Checking the evaluation identities only after multiplication']
- **limit-arbitrary-colorings-no-regularity** (formal_scope): The Ramsey property is about monochromatic congruent copies at the original scale under arbitrary finite colorings, with no measurability or regularity. Results do not transfer to rescaled copies or to colorings with extra structure. Blocked use: ['Reading the result as a statement about similar (rescaled) copies', 'Using the avoiding colorings as regular or measurable colorings']
- **limit-nonconstructive-dimension** (nonconstructive): The sufficiency direction obtains a finite ambient dimension by compactness and uses only the existence of the Hales–Jewett length. No bound on the dimension in terms of the number of colors is produced. Blocked use: ['Sizing the ambient dimension for a given number of colors', 'Quoting a quantitative forcing dimension or running time for the Ramsey property']
- **limit-ultrafilter-existence** (nonconstructive): The string realization relies on minimal compact ideals and an idempotent chosen by an existence argument in the ultrafilter semigroup. No explicit ultrafilter or implementable data structure results. Blocked use: ['Implementing the ultrafilter semigroup as a finite container', 'Extracting an explicit monochromatic copy from the construction']
- **limit-circle-five-points** (edge_case): The row-independence argument for the circle covers sets of at most five distinct points. Beyond that size the evaluation rows can no longer be independent, and generic nine-point circle sets are not Ramsey even though they are spherical. Blocked use: ['Asserting that every finite set of points on a circle is Ramsey', 'Extending the five-point interpolation argument to larger circle sets']
- **limit-quadratic-sufficient-only** (hypothesis): Linear independence of the quadratic evaluation rows over the coordinate field is only a sufficient condition for a spherical spanning set. Failure of independence says nothing about the Ramsey property. Blocked use: ['Declaring a set non-Ramsey because its quadratic rows are dependent', 'Using the rank test as a decision procedure']
- **limit-kite-transcendental** (counterexample_scope): The kite counterexample to the subtransitive characterization needs a transcendental parameter. It refutes only the necessity direction of that characterization and does not describe all Ramsey sets. Blocked use: ['Using subtransitivity as a necessary test for being Ramsey', 'Treating the kite family as typical rather than a specific counterexample family']

<a id="pm-formal"></a>
### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 172 (`lean/docs/172.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [A classification of finite Euclidean Ramsey configurations](../../preprints/A-classification-of-finite-Euclidean-Ramsey-configurations-September-23-2026/paper.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> A finite configuration is Euclidean Ramsey if every finite coloring of some sufficiently high-dimensional Euclidean space contains a monochromatic congruent copy at the original scale. The formalization gives the tensor-field classification for full-affine-span configurations, covers singleton and affine-span reductions, and proves that every Ramsey configuration is cospherical. It also proves that every nonempty subset of a finite transitive configuration and every nonempty set of at most five points on a circle is Ramsey. A further sufficient condition uses linear independence of the quadratic evaluation rows of a spherical configuration.
>
> The formalization also gives spherical non-Ramsey examples: the specified twelve-point set on the unit circle has a fifty-color obstruction in every positive dimension, and a nine-point circle configuration built from algebraically independent parameters is non-Ramsey.

Comparator source `EuclideanRamsey.lean` (1917 bytes, sha256 `cb2ede9a1f7b6c2784acfcace10de725268779be904e8b81771e79044233ac0c`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

noncomputable section
open scoped TensorProduct
namespace EuclideanRamsey

abbrev Space (d : ℕ) := EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin d)

def Congruent {s d D : ℕ} (a : Fin s → Space d) (b : Fin s → Space D) : Prop :=
  ∀ i j, dist (b i) (b j) = dist (a i) (a j)

def Ramsey {s d : ℕ} (a : Fin s → Space d) : Prop :=
  ∀ r : ℕ, 2 ≤ r → ∃ D : ℕ, 1 ≤ D ∧
    ∀ c : Space D → Fin r, ∃ b : Fin s → Space D,
      Congruent a b ∧ ∃ k : Fin r, ∀ i, c (b i) = k

def coordinateField {s d : ℕ} (a : Fin s → Space d) : IntermediateField ℚ ℝ :=
  IntermediateField.adjoin ℚ (Set.range (fun ij : Fin s × Fin d => a ij.1 ij.2))

abbrev Coeff {s d : ℕ} (a : Fin s → Space d) := ↥(coordinateField a)
abbrev TensorRing {s d : ℕ} (a : Fin s → Space d) := Coeff a ⊗[ℚ] Coeff a

def coordinate {s d : ℕ} (a : Fin s → Space d) (i : Fin s) (j : Fin d) : Coeff a :=
  ⟨a i j, IntermediateField.subset_adjoin ℚ _ (Set.mem_range_self (i, j))⟩

def augmented {s d : ℕ} (a : Fin s → Space d) (i : Fin s) : Option (Fin d) → Coeff a
  | none => 1
  | some j => coordinate a i j

def multiply {s d : ℕ} (a : Fin s → Space d) : TensorRing a →ₐ[ℚ] Coeff a :=
  Algebra.TensorProduct.lmul' ℚ

def FieldCriterion {s d : ℕ} (a : Fin s → Space d) : Prop :=
  ∃ P : Matrix (Option (Fin d)) (Option (Fin d)) (TensorRing a),
    (∀ i : Fin s, ∑ α, ∑ β,
      ((augmented a i α) ⊗ₜ[ℚ] (1 : Coeff a)) * P α β *
      ((1 : Coeff a) ⊗ₜ[ℚ] (augmented a i β)) = 0) ∧
    (∀ α β : Fin d, multiply a (P (some α) (some β)) = if α = β then 1 else 0)

theorem classification {s d : ℕ} (a : Fin s → Space d)
    (hs : 2 ≤ s) (hd : 1 ≤ d) (ha : Function.Injective a)
    (hspan : affineSpan ℝ (Set.range a) = ⊤) :
    Ramsey a ↔ FieldCriterion a := by
  sorry

end EuclideanRamsey

end

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We classify finite Euclidean Ramsey configurations by a necessary and sufficient tensor condition over their coordinate fields. The Ramsey property here concerns monochromatic congruent copies at the original scale under arbitrary finite colorings. The criterion shows that every nonempty subtransitive set and every nonempty set of at most five points on a circle is Ramsey. In particular, some Ramsey cyclic quadrilaterals are not subtransitive, disproving the necessity direction of the Leader–Russell–Walters conjectured characterization.

<a id="pm-h-2"></a>
### The classification problem

A finite nonempty set $A$ in a Euclidean space is *Ramsey* if, for every integer $r\ge2$, there is an integer $D\ge1$ such that every map $c:\mathbb R^D\to\{1,\ldots,r\}$ is constant on a congruent copy of $A$. Congruence means preservation of every pairwise distance, at the original scale. The coloring is an arbitrary function. In particular, no measurability or other regularity is assumed.

Erdős, Graham, Montgomery, Rothschild, Spencer and Straus introduced Euclidean Ramsey theory and proved that every finite Ramsey set is spherical (Erdős et al. 1973, Theorem 13) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[egm-rss](#ref-egm-rss)]. Graham later conjectured that sphericity is also sufficient (Graham 1994) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[graham-survey](#ref-graham-survey)]. Major positive results established the Ramsey property for nondegenerate triangles and then all nondegenerate simplices, by Frankl and Rödl (Frankl and Rödl 1986, 1990) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[frankl-rodl-triangles](#ref-frankl-rodl-triangles), [frankl-rodl-simplices](#ref-frankl-rodl-simplices)]; finite sets with a soluble transitive isometry group and cyclic trapezoids, by Kříž (Kříž 1991, 1992) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[kriz-permutation](#ref-kriz-permutation), [kriz-trapezoids](#ref-kriz-trapezoids)]; and vertex sets of regular polytopes, by Cantwell (Cantwell 2007) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[cantwell-polytopes](#ref-cantwell-polytopes)].

Leader, Russell and Walters proposed a different characterization: a finite set is Ramsey exactly when it embeds in a finite transitive Euclidean set (Leader, Russell, and Walters 2012, Conjecture A) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[lrw-transitive](#ref-lrw-transitive)]. Transitivity means that the isometries of the finite set act transitively on its points. An isometric subset of such a set is called *subtransitive*. Their group-theoretic and Hales–Jewett formulations led to the Block Sets Conjecture; subsequent work studies the possible block sizes (Ivan, Leader, and Walters 2026a) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[ilw-blocks](#ref-ilw-blocks)]. The link with soluble symmetry has also expanded: Karamanlis embedded every simplex in a finite product of regular polygons (Karamanlis 2022) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[karamanlis-tori](#ref-karamanlis-tori)], and Behague constructed soluble transitive extensions for many known Ramsey classes and further permutation configurations (Behague 2025) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[behague-subsoluble](#ref-behague-subsoluble)]. Ivan, Leader and Walters developed generalized-prism constructions, with Ramsey conclusions under a soluble symmetry hypothesis (Ivan, Leader, and Walters 2026b) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[ilw-prisms](#ref-ilw-prisms)]. Moore and, independently, Mirabi proved that adjoining a point outside the affine span of a Ramsey set preserves the Ramsey property (Moore 2026; Mirabi 2026) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[moore-pyramid](#ref-moore-pyramid), [mirabi-extensions](#ref-mirabi-extensions)]. The affine-span restriction leaves the case of arbitrary coplanar circle points beyond this extension theorem.

Pálvölgyi has recently given a seven-point circle configuration that is not Ramsey, disproving the spherical conjecture (Pálvölgyi 2026, Theorem 1) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[palvolgyi-heptagon](#ref-palvolgyi-heptagon)]. The appendix to that version also states that generic seven-point circle configurations are not Ramsey. Its author’s note announces a proof that all finite transitive sets are Ramsey and says that its exposition is still being prepared.[^1] Our main theorem gives a necessary and sufficient algebraic condition for an arbitrary finite Euclidean configuration.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### The exact tensor criterion

The problem is to characterize these finite sets up to congruence. A singleton is Ramsey. For any other set, let $d\ge1$ be its affine dimension, and choose a congruent representative $A=\{a_1,\ldots,a_s\}\subset\mathbb R^d$ whose affine span is $\mathbb R^d$. The points are distinct and $s\ge2$. Define $$p_i=\begin{pmatrix}1\\a_i\end{pmatrix},\qquad
 F=\mathbb Q\bigl((a_i)_\alpha:1\le i\le s,\ 1\le\alpha\le d\bigr),
 \qquad B=F\otimes_{\mathbb Q}F.$$ The commutative ring $B$ has a multiplication homomorphism $$m_F:B\longrightarrow F,\qquad m_F(x\otimes y)=xy.$$ An equality in $B$ is stronger than the equality obtained by applying $m_F$; this map need not be injective. Index the entries of $p_i$ and the rows and columns of a $(d+1)$-by-$(d+1)$ matrix by $0,1,\ldots,d$. Thus index $0$ is the constant coordinate.

<a id="stmt-thm-classification"></a>
<div id="thm:classification" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1** (Classification). *Let $A=\{a_1,\ldots,a_s\}\subset\mathbb R^d$ be a finite set of at least two points with affine span $\mathbb R^d$, and define $p_i,F,B,m_F$ as above. Then $A$ is Ramsey if and only if there is a matrix $P\in\operatorname{Mat}_{d+1}(B)$ such that <a id="eq:field-evaluations"></a><a id="eq:field-spatial"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 (p_i\otimes1)^T P(1\otimes p_i)&=0
       &&(1\le i\le s),\label{eq:field-evaluations}\\
 m_F(P_{\alpha\beta})&=\delta_{\alpha\beta}
       &&(1\le\alpha,\beta\le d).\label{eq:field-spatial}
\end{aligned}$$ Here the entries of $p_i\otimes1$ and $1\otimes p_i$ are formed componentwise. There are no separate conditions on the multiplied constant row or constant column of $P$.*

</div>

The condition is independent of the chosen congruent representative in its affine dimension; we also prove this directly through its tensor formulation. It uses exact relations in the coordinate field. It is not a procedure for recovering those relations from numerical approximations to arbitrary real coordinates.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Geometric consequences

Applying $m_F$ to the evaluation equations gives an ordinary sphere equation $$\|a_i\|^2+\ell\cdot a_i+c=0\qquad(1\le i\le s)$$ for some $\ell\in F^d$ and $c\in F$. The tensor condition requires the evaluation identities to hold before multiplication. This is the distinction between sphericity and the Ramsey property that the classification detects.

Every subtransitive set satisfies the tensor condition. Another sufficient condition is that evaluation at the points of a spherical set gives independent functionals on the space of quadratic polynomials. It applies to every nonempty set of at most five points on a circle, and hence to every cyclic quadrilateral. In Corollary <a href="#cons:kites" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cons:kites">23</a>, we combine this result with the non-subtransitive cyclic kites of Leader, Russell and Walters (Leader, Russell, and Walters 2011, Corollary 2) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[lrw-kites](#ref-lrw-kites)]. Those kites are Ramsey, disproving the necessity direction of their proposed characterization and their non-Ramseyness conjecture for the same family (Leader, Russell, and Walters 2011, Conjecture 3) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[lrw-kites](#ref-lrw-kites)].

For negative examples, nine points obtained from algebraically independent real parameters in a rational parametrization of a circle fail the tensor condition. Pálvölgyi’s generic seven-point claim (Pálvölgyi 2026, Theorem A.3) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[palvolgyi-heptagon](#ref-palvolgyi-heptagon)] already implies this non-Ramseyness conclusion; our determinant calculation illustrates how to apply the classification directly. We also give a tensor obstruction for a twelve-point configuration consisting of three rotated coordinate squares, using the same type of weighted derivation moments as his heptagon construction. These consequences are proved after the classification and require no further Ramsey construction.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### How the algebra forces a monochromatic copy

The proof begins by replacing the coordinate-field matrix with a finite rational combination of tensors of affine functions on $\mathbb R^d$. Its evaluations at all points of $A$ vanish as rational tensors, while its gradient Gram matrix is $I_d$. If no such tensor exists, algebraic separation produces a finite coloring that avoids $A$ in every dimension. This proves necessity.

For sufficiency, the difficulty is to convert the tensor cancellations into a congruent copy at scale one: arbitrary colorings provide no continuity with which to absorb approximation errors. The proof has three stages.

First, the tensor determines signed rational multiplicities for a finite list of affine functions. At every point of $A$, the positive and negative multiplicities cancel separately at each evaluation value. A Gaussian construction, followed by discretization and rational approximation within a fixed convolution image, makes the negative gradient second moment small while retaining these cancellations exactly. The positive and negative multiplicities then give two lists of affine functions. Evaluating the lists at $a_i$ gives vectors $f_i,g_i$ with $$\|f_i-f_j\|^2=\|a_i-a_j\|^2,\qquad
 \|g_i-g_j\|^2=q\|a_i-a_j\|^2,$$ where $q>0$ can be arbitrarily small, and each $g_i$ is a coordinate permutation of $f_i$. Both the distances and the matching coordinate multisets are exact.

Second, we associate a free-group generator to each real sequence with finite support. A *path* is an ordered product of $s$-tuples: each factor either records an ordered scaled copy of $A$ or has the same group element in every position. Its *endpoint* is the coordinatewise product, and its *weight* is the sum of the squared copy scales. We prove that equal endpoints with arbitrarily small weight ratios force the Ramsey property. The obstacle, familiar from the group and word methods of Leader, Russell and Walters (Leader, Russell, and Walters 2012, secs. 2–3) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[lrw-transitive](#ref-lrw-transitive)], is the initially unknown number $t$ of variable positions in a Hales–Jewett line. After fixing the Hales–Jewett word length $n$, we prepare one endpoint at weights $1,1/2,\ldots,1/n$ before coloring the words. An ultrafilter construction then realizes the products by finite strings, keeping common factors identical. The $t$ variable positions give squared-distance multiplier $t(1/t)=1$. Compactness supplies a finite ambient dimension for each number of colors.

Third, the permutation pairs from the first stage must be made to have equal path endpoints. Permutation averaging puts the discrepancies between their group entries in a subgroup whose elements can be corrected by appending paths of unit-scale copies. Further averaging makes the total correction weight small relative to the larger original weight. Appending these corrections produces the equal endpoints and arbitrarily small weight ratios required in the second stage.

Sections <a href="#sec:tensors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:tensors">2</a>–<a href="#pairs:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pairs:section">4</a> establish the algebraic formulation, necessity, and the exact pair construction. Section <a href="#paths:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="paths:section">5</a> states and proves the group-endpoint criterion that suffices for Ramsey forcing. Section <a href="#sec:groups" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:groups">6</a> constructs the required endpoints and completes the proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:classification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:classification">1</a>. Section <a href="#cons:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cons:section">7</a> derives the geometric examples. All arguments take place in ZFC.

<a id="sec:tensors"></a>
### Rational tensors and the coloring obstruction

We first express the matrix condition of Theorem <a href="#thm:classification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:classification">1</a> as a condition on affine functions. This formulation makes the obstruction to arbitrary colorings explicit and supplies the input for the construction in the next section. Throughout, $s\geq2$, $d\geq1$, and $A=\{a_1,\ldots,a_s\}$ affinely spans $\mathbb R^d$; the field $F$, ring $B$, columns $p_i$, and multiplication map $m_F$ are as in the theorem.

<a id="alg:tensor"></a>
Put $W=\mathbb R\times\mathbb R^d$, regarded as a vector space over $\mathbb Q$. An element $w=(v,u)$ specifies the affine function $a\mapsto v+a\cdot u$. For each label define the $\mathbb Q$-linear evaluation map $$e_i:W\longrightarrow\mathbb R,\qquad e_i(v,u)=v+a_i\cdot u.$$ Let $\mathcal T(W)$ be the subspace of $W\otimes_{\mathbb Q}W$ fixed by the flip $w\otimes w'\mapsto w'\otimes w$. Thus our symmetric tensors are flip-invariant tensors. The map $$(v,u)\otimes(v',u')\longmapsto u(u')^{\mathsf T}$$ is $\mathbb Q$-linear on the tensor product and restricts to a map $G:\mathcal T(W)\to\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}_d(\mathbb R)$, where $\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}_d(\mathbb R)$ denotes the real symmetric matrices. In particular, $G(w\otimes w)=uu^{\mathsf T}$. The tensor condition is the existence of $T\in\mathcal T(W)$ such that $$\label{alg:tensor}
 (e_i\otimes e_i)(T)=0\quad(1\leq i\leq s),
 \qquad G(T)=I_d.$$ The first identities hold in $\mathbb R\otimes_{\mathbb Q}\mathbb R$. They are stronger than their images under real multiplication.

<a id="stmt-alg-equivalence"></a>
<div id="alg:equivalence" class="proposition">

**Proposition 2**. *The matrix condition in Theorem <a href="#thm:classification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:classification">1</a> is equivalent to <a href="#alg:tensor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alg:tensor">[alg:tensor]</a>.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-alg-equivalence"><summary>Proof of stmt-alg-equivalence</summary>

*Proof.* Write $\mathscr B=\mathbb R\otimes_{\mathbb Q}\mathbb R$, let $\tau(x\otimes y)=y\otimes x$, and let $m_{\mathbb R}:\mathscr B\to\mathbb R$ be multiplication. The coordinate decomposition of $W$ identifies $W\otimes_{\mathbb Q}W$ with arrays $Q=(Q_{\alpha\beta})_{0\leq\alpha,\beta\leq d}$ over $\mathscr B$. Under this identification, evaluation is $$E_i(Q)=\sum_{\alpha,\beta=0}^d
 (p_{i,\alpha}\otimes1)Q_{\alpha\beta}
 (1\otimes p_{i,\beta}),$$ and the gradient matrix is the spatial block $(m_{\mathbb R}(Q_{\alpha\beta}))_{1\leq\alpha,\beta\leq d}$. Tensor flip becomes $$Q^*_{\alpha\beta}=\tau(Q_{\beta\alpha}).$$ Directly from the evaluation formula, $E_i(Q^*)=\tau(E_i(Q))$, and multiplication of $Q^*$ transposes the multiplied array. Consequently $\tfrac12(Q+Q^*)$ preserves every zero evaluation and any multiplied spatial block equal to $I_d$. This proves the implication from a matrix over $B$ to a symmetric tensor, using the natural inclusion $B\subset\mathscr B$.

<a id="alg:free-expansion"></a>
For the converse, we show that extending the coefficient ring from $B$ to $\mathscr B$ introduces no new solutions. Choose an $F$-basis $(r_\lambda)_{\lambda\in\Lambda}$ of $\mathbb R$. Distributing tensor products over direct sums gives the free $B$-module decomposition $$\label{alg:free-expansion}
 \mathscr B=
 \bigoplus_{\lambda,\mu\in\Lambda}
 B(r_\lambda\otimes r_\mu).$$ Here a coefficient $x\otimes y\in B$ in the indicated summand represents $(xr_\lambda)\otimes(yr_\mu)$. If an array $Q$ over $\mathscr B$ solves the evaluation equations and has multiplied spatial block $I_d$, its entries have a common finite expansion $$Q=\sum_{(\lambda,\mu)\in J}
 (r_\lambda\otimes r_\mu)P^{\lambda\mu},
 \qquad P^{\lambda\mu}\in\operatorname{Mat}_{d+1}(B).$$ All coefficients in the maps $E_i$ belong to $B$. Thus <a href="#alg:free-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alg:free-expansion">[alg:free-expansion]</a> implies $E_i(P^{\lambda\mu})=0$ for each pair and each label. Let $C^{\lambda\mu}\in\operatorname{Mat}_d(F)$ be the multiplied spatial block of $P^{\lambda\mu}$. Applying multiplication to the expansion gives $$I_d=\sum_{(\lambda,\mu)\in J}
 r_\lambda r_\mu C^{\lambda\mu}.$$ This finite linear system has coefficients and right-hand side in $F$. Since it is consistent over $\mathbb R$, row reduction over $F$ shows that it is consistent over $F$. Choose $c_{\lambda\mu}\in F$ with $I_d=\sum c_{\lambda\mu}C^{\lambda\mu}$. Then $$P=\sum_{(\lambda,\mu)\in J}
 (c_{\lambda\mu}\otimes1)P^{\lambda\mu}$$ is a solution over $B$. The individual matrices $C^{\lambda\mu}$ need not be symmetric; the descent took place in the full space $\operatorname{Mat}_d(F)$. If a symmetric tensor is wanted after descent, the symmetrization already proved applies to $P$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-alg-noetherian"></a>
<div id="alg:noetherian" class="remark">

*Remark 3*. The finite coordinate presentation explains the algebraic nature of the criterion. Let $F_0=\mathbb Q[(a_i)_j]$, the subring of $\mathbb R$ generated by all coordinates. It is a finitely generated domain and $F=\operatorname{Frac}(F_0)$. The ring $B$ is the localization of $F\otimes_{\mathbb Q}F_0$ at the elements $1\otimes f$ for $0\ne f\in F_0$. The latter ring is a finitely generated $F$-algebra, so $B$ is Noetherian. The kernel of the finite evaluation system therefore has finitely many $B$-module generators. Its multiplied spatial image is the $F$-span of their multiplied spatial blocks, because every scalar $c\in F$ lifts to $c\otimes1$. This description uses exact coordinate-field relations; it makes no claim to recover those relations from numerical real inputs.

</div>

<a id="stmt-alg-congruence"></a>
<div id="alg:congruence" class="remark">

*Remark 4*. The tensor condition is directly invariant under congruence. If $a_i'=Qa_i+t$ with $Q\in O(d)$, the invertible $\mathbb Q$-linear map $$H:W\longrightarrow W,\qquad
 H(v,u)=(v-t\cdot Qu,Qu)$$ satisfies $e_i'\circ H=e_i$ and $G((H\otimes H)T)=QG(T)Q^{\mathsf T}$. It therefore carries certificates for $A$ to certificates for $A'$; the inverse congruence gives the converse. Reordering labels merely reorders the equations. Any two congruent representatives in their affine dimension are related by such a map, so Proposition <a href="#alg:equivalence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="alg:equivalence">2</a> also proves representative independence for the matrix criterion, even when their coordinate fields differ.

</div>

We next prove the necessary direction of the classification. Failure of <a href="#alg:tensor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alg:tensor">[alg:tensor]</a> gives an algebraic functional, and its values provide a fixed finite coloring that works in every ambient dimension. The invariant-and-coloring strategy goes back to Erdős et al. (Erdős et al. 1973, Theorem 13 and Lemma 15) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[egm-rss](#ref-egm-rss)]; here rational tensor separation supplies the invariant, and the modular coloring is proved directly. Their real-variable coloring lemma extends Rado’s work on partition regularity (Rado 1945) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[rado-note](#ref-rado-note)].

<a id="stmt-alg-necessity"></a>
<div id="alg:necessity" class="proposition">

**Proposition 5**. *If <a href="#alg:tensor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alg:tensor">[alg:tensor]</a> has no solution, then there is an integer $r_0\geq2$ such that every $\mathbb R^D$ admits an $r_0$-coloring with no monochromatic congruent copy of $A$. In particular, every Euclidean Ramsey set satisfies the matrix condition of Theorem <a href="#thm:classification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:classification">1</a>.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-alg-necessity"><summary>Proof of stmt-alg-necessity</summary>

<a id="alg:separated"></a>
<a id="alg:constant-cancellation"></a>
*Proof.* Regard $$\mathcal Y=(\mathbb R\otimes_{\mathbb Q}\mathbb R)^s\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}_d(\mathbb R)$$ as a $\mathbb Q$-vector space and define the $\mathbb Q$-linear map $$\Phi:\mathcal T(W)\longrightarrow\mathcal Y,
 \qquad
 \Phi(T)=\bigl((e_i\otimes e_i)(T)\bigr)_{i=1}^s\oplus G(T).$$ By assumption $y_0=(0,\ldots,0,I_d)$ is outside its image. In the quotient vector space $\mathcal Y/\operatorname{im}\Phi$, extend the nonzero class of $y_0$ to a basis over $\mathbb Q$. Sending that basis element to $1$ and the others to $0$ gives a $\mathbb Q$-linear functional $\ell:\mathcal Y\to\mathbb R$ such that $$\ell\circ\Phi=0,\qquad \ell(y_0)=1.$$ Write its components as $\ell_i:\mathbb R\otimes_{\mathbb Q}\mathbb R\to\mathbb R$ and $L:\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}_d(\mathbb R)\to\mathbb R$, and put $h_i(x)=\ell_i(x\otimes x)$. Applying $\ell$ to $\Phi(w\otimes w)$ gives $$\label{alg:separated}
 \sum_{i=1}^s h_i(v+a_i\cdot u)+L(uu^{\mathsf T})=0,
 \qquad L(I_d)=1.$$ Taking $u=0$ shows that $$\label{alg:constant-cancellation}
 \sum_{i=1}^s h_i(x)=0\qquad(x\in\mathbb R).$$ These are purely algebraic functionals; no continuity or measurability is asserted or needed.

<a id="alg:copy-obstruction"></a>
Fix $D$ and define $H_i(z)=\sum_{j=1}^D h_i(z_j)$ for $z\in\mathbb R^D$. We claim that every ordered congruent copy $(b_1,\ldots,b_s)$ satisfies $$\label{alg:copy-obstruction}
 \sum_{i=1}^s H_i(b_i)=-1.$$ Indeed, equality of the pairwise distances gives equality of the Gram matrices of $(a_i-a_1)_i$ and $(b_i-b_1)_i$ by polarization. Thus sending each $a_i-a_1$ to $b_i-b_1$ defines an inner-product-preserving linear map on their span: any relation among the first differences has squared norm zero for the corresponding combination of the second. Since $A$ affinely spans $\mathbb R^d$, there are a linear isometry $Q:\mathbb R^d\to\mathbb R^D$ and $t\in\mathbb R^D$ such that $b_i=t+Qa_i$. Writing $u_j^{\mathsf T}$ for the $j$th row of $Q$, we have $$(b_i)_j=e_i(t_j,u_j),\qquad
 \sum_{j=1}^D u_ju_j^{\mathsf T}=Q^{\mathsf T}Q=I_d.$$ Sum <a href="#alg:separated" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alg:separated">[alg:separated]</a> over $j$. The finite additivity of $L$ proves <a href="#alg:copy-obstruction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alg:copy-obstruction">[alg:copy-obstruction]</a>. Also <a href="#alg:constant-cancellation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alg:constant-cancellation">[alg:constant-cancellation]</a> gives $\sum_iH_i(z)=0$ at every single point $z$.

Choose $K=2s+1$ and partition $[0,2)$ into $K$ half-open intervals of equal length $2/K<1/s$. Color a point $z$ by the $s$-tuple of interval labels of the representatives of $H_i(z)$ modulo $2$. This uses at most $r_0=K^s$ colors, independently of $D$. If $b_1,\ldots,b_s$ were monochromatic, there would be integers $n_i$ and real numbers $\varepsilon_i$, with $|\varepsilon_i|<2/K$, such that $$H_i(b_i)-H_i(b_1)=2n_i+\varepsilon_i.$$ Summation yields $$-1=2\sum_i n_i+\sum_i\varepsilon_i,
 \qquad \left|\sum_i\varepsilon_i\right|<1.$$ The first identity makes the error sum an odd integer, contradicting the second. This coloring avoids $A$ in every dimension and completes the proof. ◻

</details>

The remaining direction begins with a tensor satisfying <a href="#alg:tensor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alg:tensor">[alg:tensor]</a>. Its evaluations vanish before multiplication, and that exact cancellation will produce equal coordinate histograms for two embeddings at different scales.

<a id="stencil:section"></a>
### A finite lattice identity

Assume that the tensor condition holds. We begin the sufficiency proof by turning its rational tensor equalities into exact cancellation identities for finitely many affine functions. These identities will let us construct two copies of the configuration at different scales whose corresponding points have the same coordinates in different orders.

<a id="stencil:gram"></a>
Recall that $W=\mathbb R\times\mathbb R^d$ is viewed over $\mathbb Q$, and that $e_i(v,u)=v+a_i\cdot u$. Let $T\in W\otimes_{\mathbb Q}W$ be symmetric, with $$(e_i\otimes e_i)T=0\quad(1\leq i\leq s),
 \qquad G(T)=I_d.$$ A tensor uses only finitely many vectors. Choose a basis $w_1,\ldots,w_k$ over $\mathbb Q$ for a finite-dimensional subspace containing its factors, and write $$T=\sum_{a,b=1}^k C_{ab}w_a\otimes w_b,
 \qquad C\in\operatorname{Mat}_k(\mathbb Q),\quad C=C^{\mathsf T}.$$ For $\lambda\in\mathbb Q^k$, put $$w(\lambda)=\sum_{j=1}^k\lambda_jw_j.$$ Write $w_j=(v_j,u_j)$ and let $U=(u_1\ \cdots\ u_k)$ be the real $d\times k$ matrix whose columns are their gradients. The gradient identity for $T$ is $$\label{stencil:gram}
 UCU^{\mathsf T}=I_d.$$

Set $\Gamma=\mathbb Z^k$ and define the evaluation lattices $$\Lambda_i=\{\lambda\in\Gamma:e_i(w(\lambda))=0\},
 \qquad V_i=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb Q}\Lambda_i.$$ Clearing denominators shows that $V_i$ is the kernel of the rational linear map $\lambda\mapsto e_i(w(\lambda))$ on $\mathbb Q^k$. Thus $\mathbb Q^k/V_i$ embeds into $\mathbb R$ over $\mathbb Q$. Tensoring this injection with itself remains injective, so the tensor represented by $C$ has zero image in $(\mathbb Q^k/V_i)\otimes_{\mathbb Q}(\mathbb Q^k/V_i)$.

The next lemma converts these tensor equalities into a finitely supported rational function on $\Gamma$. Vanishing of its sums on each coset will record exact cancellation among equal evaluations.

<a id="stmt-stencil-finite"></a>
<div id="stencil:finite" class="lemma">

<a id="stencil:cosets"></a>
<a id="stencil:moments"></a>
**Lemma 6** (Finite lattice identity). *Let $k,s\geq1$, let $\Lambda_1,\ldots,\Lambda_s$ be subgroups of $\Gamma=\mathbb Z^k$, and put $V_i=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb Q}\Lambda_i$. Let $C\in\operatorname{Mat}_k(\mathbb Q)$ be symmetric. Suppose that the image of $\sum_{a,b}C_{ab}\varepsilon_a\otimes\varepsilon_b$ in $(\mathbb Q^k/V_i)\otimes_{\mathbb Q}(\mathbb Q^k/V_i)$ is zero for every $i$, where $\varepsilon_1,\ldots,\varepsilon_k$ is the standard basis of $\mathbb Q^k$. There is a finitely supported function $\nu:\Gamma\to\mathbb Q$ such that $$\label{stencil:cosets}
 \sum_{\gamma\in q}\nu(\gamma)=0
 \qquad(q\in\Gamma/\Lambda_i,\ 1\leq i\leq s)$$ and $$\label{stencil:moments}
 \sum_\gamma\nu(\gamma)=0,\qquad
 \sum_\gamma\nu(\gamma)\gamma=0,\qquad
 \sum_\gamma\nu(\gamma)\gamma\gamma^{\mathsf T}=C.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-stencil-finite"><summary>Proof of stmt-stencil-finite</summary>

*Proof.* The Laurent polynomial ring $$R=\mathbb Q[X_1^{\pm1},\ldots,X_k^{\pm1}]$$ is the group algebra of $\Gamma$, with $X^\gamma=\prod_jX_j^{\gamma_j}$. The quotient map $\Gamma\to\Gamma/\Lambda_i$ induces a ring homomorphism whose kernel is $$J_i=(X^h-1:h\in\Lambda_i).$$ Indeed, its kernel consists of Laurent polynomials with zero coefficient sum on each coset. Within a coset, choose a representative $\gamma_0$ from the finite support; a zero-sum polynomial on that coset is a sum of terms $c_\gamma(X^\gamma-X^{\gamma_0})$, each in $J_i$.

<a id="stencil:linear-ideals"></a>
We will find a Laurent polynomial in every $J_i$ with prescribed terms through degree two near $X=(1,\ldots,1)$. To use the tensor hypothesis, we pass to formal logarithmic coordinates, in which the generators $X^h-1$ become linear forms multiplied by units. Put $\mathfrak m=(X_1-1,\ldots,X_k-1)$ and complete $R$ with respect to $\mathfrak m$. With $Z_j=X_j-1$, its completion is $\widehat R=\mathbb Q[[Z_1,\ldots,Z_k]]$: the factors $1+Z_j$ are already invertible modulo each power of $(Z_1,\ldots,Z_k)$. The formal changes of variables $$Y_j=\log(1+Z_j),\qquad Z_j=\exp(Y_j)-1$$ identify this ring with $\mathbb Q[[Y_1,\ldots,Y_k]]$ and identify $\mathfrak m\widehat R$ with $(Y_1,\ldots,Y_k)$. For each $h\in\Gamma$, $$X^h-1=\exp(h\cdot Y)-1
       =(h\cdot Y)\sum_{n\geq0}\frac{(h\cdot Y)^n}{(n+1)!}.$$ The final factor is a unit. Hence $$\label{stencil:linear-ideals}
 J_i\widehat R=(h\cdot Y:h\in\Lambda_i)\widehat R.$$

Consider the quadratic polynomial $$Q_2(Y)=\tfrac12Y^{\mathsf T}CY.$$ Identify $\mathbb Q[Y_1,\ldots,Y_k]$ with the symmetric algebra of $\mathbb Q^k$ by sending $\varepsilon_j$ to $Y_j$. The map to the symmetric algebra of $\mathbb Q^k/V_i$ has kernel generated by $V_i$ in degree one. This follows, for example, by extending a basis of $V_i$ to a basis of $\mathbb Q^k$. The tensor hypothesis therefore puts $Q_2$ in the ideal generated by the linear forms $h\cdot Y$, $h\in\Lambda_i$. By <a href="#stencil:linear-ideals" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stencil:linear-ideals">[stencil:linear-ideals]</a>, $Q_2\in J_i\widehat R$ for every $i$.

The ring $R$ is Noetherian, being a localization of a polynomial ring over $\mathbb Q$. Its $\mathfrak m$-adic completion is therefore flat over $R$ (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, Lemma 10.97.2, Tag 00MB) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[stacks-completion](#ref-stacks-completion)]. If $I=\bigcap_{i=1}^sJ_i$, the injection $$R/I\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{i=1}^sR/J_i$$ remains injective after tensoring with $\widehat R$. Consequently $$I\widehat R=\bigcap_{i=1}^sJ_i\widehat R,$$ and $Q_2\in I\widehat R$.

We now recover a finite Laurent polynomial while retaining exact membership in $I$. Write $$Q_2=\sum_{a=1}^N f_a\alpha_a,
 \qquad f_a\in I,\quad\alpha_a\in\widehat R,$$ as a finite sum. For each $a$, truncate the formal $Z$-expansion of $\alpha_a$ through degree two to obtain $b_a\in R$ with $b_a-\alpha_a\in\mathfrak m^3\widehat R$. Then $f=\sum_af_ab_a\in I$ and $$f(e^{Y_1},\ldots,e^{Y_k})
 =\tfrac12Y^{\mathsf T}CY\pmod{(Y_1,\ldots,Y_k)^3}.$$ Write the finite Laurent expansion as $f=\sum_\gamma\nu(\gamma)X^\gamma$. Membership in each $J_i$ gives <a href="#stencil:cosets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stencil:cosets">[stencil:cosets]</a>. Finally, $$X^\gamma=\exp(\gamma\cdot Y)
 =1+\gamma\cdot Y+\tfrac12(\gamma\cdot Y)^2\pmod{(Y)^3}.$$ Comparing the constant term, gradient, and Hessian at $Y=0$ gives <a href="#stencil:moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stencil:moments">[stencil:moments]</a>. ◻

</details>

Apply Lemma <a href="#stencil:finite" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="stencil:finite">6</a> to the matrix and evaluation lattices constructed above. If $\gamma,\gamma'\in\Gamma$, then $e_i(w(\gamma))=e_i(w(\gamma'))$ exactly when $\gamma-\gamma'\in\Lambda_i$. Thus <a href="#stencil:cosets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stencil:cosets">[stencil:cosets]</a> says that the positive and negative weights cancel separately at every evaluation value. The second moment in <a href="#stencil:moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stencil:moments">[stencil:moments]</a>, together with <a href="#stencil:gram" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stencil:gram">[stencil:gram]</a>, gives their prescribed difference in gradient Gram matrices. We next redistribute these weights so that the negative part has arbitrarily small gradient second moment, while all these equalities remain exact.

<a id="pairs:section"></a>
### Exact coordinate permutations at two scales

The tensor condition will now produce two configurations with the same coordinate values at each label, but with arbitrarily different scales. The equality of coordinate values is an equality of finite multisets, including multiplicities.

<a id="stmt-pairs-two-scale"></a>
<div id="pairs:two-scale" class="lemma">

<a id="pairs:distances"></a>
**Lemma 7** (Two-scale configurations). *Let $A=\{a_1,\ldots,a_s\}\subset\mathbb R^d$ have affine span $\mathbb R^d$, with $s\geq2$. Let $W=\mathbb R^{1+d}$ be regarded as a vector space over $\mathbb Q$. Suppose that a symmetric tensor $T\in W\otimes_{\mathbb Q}W$ satisfies $$(e_i\otimes e_i)(T)=0\quad(1\leq i\leq s),
 \qquad G(T)=I_d,$$ where $e_i(v,u)=v+a_i\cdot u$. For every $\eta>0$, there are an integer $\ell\geq1$ and vectors $f_i,g_i\in\mathbb R^\ell$ such that $$\begin{aligned}
 \|f_i-f_j\|^2&=\|a_i-a_j\|^2,
 &\|g_i-g_j\|^2&=q\|a_i-a_j\|^2,
 &q&=\frac{\eta}{1+\eta}, \label{pairs:distances}
\end{aligned}$$ for every $i,j$, and $g_i$ is a permutation of the coordinates of $f_i$ for each $i$.*

</div>

The finite function $\nu$ from Lemma <a href="#stencil:finite" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="stencil:finite">6</a> supplies signed rational weights whose sums vanish on every evaluation class. Positive and negative weights can therefore become two lists of coordinates with identical multisets of values. Their second moments control the difference of their gradient Gram matrices. The remaining task is to make the negative list’s Gram matrix arbitrarily small. We first obtain the required inequality by smoothing, and then obtain finite rational weights while preserving all cancellation identities exactly.

For a real symmetric $k\times k$ matrix $C$, write $$D_C=\frac12\sum_{j,l=1}^k C_{jl}\partial_j\partial_l,
 \qquad r_+ = \max\{r,0\},\quad r_- = \max\{-r,0\}\quad(r\in\mathbb R).$$

<a id="stmt-pairs-bump"></a>
<div id="pairs:bump" class="lemma">

<a id="pairs:bump-bound"></a>
**Lemma 8** (A compactly supported smoothing function). *Let $C$ be a real symmetric $k\times k$ matrix and let $U\in\operatorname{Mat}_{d\times k}(\mathbb R)$, where $d\geq1$ and $UCU^{\mathsf T}=I_d$. For every $\eta>0$, there is a nonnegative $b\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^k)$ such that $$\label{pairs:bump-bound}
 \int_{\mathbb R^k}b(x)\,dx>0,
 \qquad
 \int_{\mathbb R^k}(D_Cb)_-(x)\|Ux\|^2\,dx
 <\eta\int_{\mathbb R^k}b(x)\,dx.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pairs-bump"><summary>Proof of stmt-pairs-bump</summary>

*Proof.* We will average Gaussian densities with covariance matrices running from $S$ to $S+C$. We first construct a positive definite $S$ for which $S+C$ is positive definite and $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(USU^{\mathsf T})$ is small.

<a id="pairs:small-covariance"></a>
The identity $UCU^{\mathsf T}=I_d$ implies that $U$ has rank $d$. Set $H=\operatorname{im}(U^{\mathsf T})$ and $V=\ker U$, so that $\mathbb R^k=H\oplus V$ orthogonally. The restriction of the quadratic form $C$ to $H$ is positive definite: if $0\ne h=U^{\mathsf T}y$, then $$h^{\mathsf T}Ch=y^{\mathsf T}UCU^{\mathsf T}y=\|y\|^2>0.$$ Choose $\epsilon>0$ with $\epsilon\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(UU^{\mathsf T})<\eta/8$. We claim that, for a sufficiently large $L>0$, the matrix $$S=\epsilon P_H+L P_V$$ is positive definite and so is $S+C$. Here $P_H,P_V$ are the orthogonal projections. To check the latter assertion, write $$S+C=
 \begin{pmatrix}
  A&B\\ B^{\mathsf T}&C_{VV}+L I_V
 \end{pmatrix},
 \qquad A=C_{HH}+\epsilon I_H>0.$$ Choose $L$ such that $C_{VV}+L I_V-B^{\mathsf T}A^{-1}B>0$ and use the Schur complement. If $V=\{0\}$, the second block is absent. Since $UP_V=0$, this construction also gives $$\label{pairs:small-covariance}
 \mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(USU^{\mathsf T})=\epsilon\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(UU^{\mathsf T})<\eta/8.$$ Every matrix $S+tC$, $0\leq t\leq1$, is positive definite, being a convex combination of $S$ and $S+C$.

<a id="pairs:gaussian-bound"></a>
For a positive definite $k\times k$ matrix $Q$, let $$\phi_Q(x)=(2\pi)^{-k/2}(\det Q)^{-1/2}
                 \exp\!\left(-\frac12x^{\mathsf T}Q^{-1}x\right)$$ be the centered Gaussian density with covariance $Q$. Differentiating the displayed formula, with $Q_t=S+tC$, gives $$\frac{d}{dt}\phi_{Q_t}(x)
 =\frac12\left(
    x^{\mathsf T}Q_t^{-1}CQ_t^{-1}x-\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(Q_t^{-1}C)
   \right)\phi_{Q_t}(x)
 =D_C\phi_{Q_t}(x).$$ This identity does not require $C$ to be positive semidefinite. The covariances $Q_t$ have uniformly bounded eigenvalues, bounded away from zero, so the densities and each of their spatial derivatives have uniform Gaussian decay. Consequently $$b_*(x)=\int_0^1\phi_{S+tC}(x)\,dt$$ is a nonnegative Schwartz function of integral $1$, and differentiation under the integral gives $$D_Cb_* = \phi_{S+C}-\phi_S.$$ The pointwise inequality $(\phi_{S+C}-\phi_S)_-\leq\phi_S$ and <a href="#pairs:small-covariance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pairs:small-covariance">[pairs:small-covariance]</a> imply $$\label{pairs:gaussian-bound}
 \int_{\mathbb R^k}(D_Cb_*)_-(x)\|Ux\|^2\,dx
 \leq\int_{\mathbb R^k}\phi_S(x)\|Ux\|^2\,dx
 =\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(USU^{\mathsf T})<\eta/8.$$

To make the support compact, choose a smooth function $\chi$ with $0\leq\chi\leq1$, equal to $1$ on the unit ball and zero outside the ball of radius $2$. Put $\chi_R(x)=\chi(x/R)$ and $b_R=\chi_Rb_*$. Then $\int b_R\to1$. The product rule reads $$D_Cb_R=\chi_R D_Cb_*
  +\sum_{j,l}C_{jl}(\partial_j\chi_R)(\partial_lb_*)
  +b_*D_C\chi_R.$$ The derivatives of $\chi_R$ have bounds of orders $R^{-1}$ and $R^{-2}$ and are supported where $R\leq\|x\|\leq2R$. The Schwartz decay therefore implies $$\int_{\mathbb R^k}|D_Cb_R-D_Cb_*|(x)\|Ux\|^2\,dx\longrightarrow0.$$ Since $r\mapsto r_-$ is $1$-Lipschitz, the corresponding weighted negative-part integrals converge as well. For sufficiently large $R$, we have $\int b_R>1/2$ and the last displayed integral is less than $\eta/8$. Equation <a href="#pairs:gaussian-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pairs:gaussian-bound">[pairs:gaussian-bound]</a> now gives $$\int(D_Cb_R)_-\|Ux\|^2<\eta/4<\eta\int b_R.$$ Taking $b=b_R$ proves the lemma. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-pairs-rational"></a>
<div id="pairs:rational" class="lemma">

<a id="pairs:exact-moment"></a>
<a id="pairs:small-negative"></a>
**Lemma 9** (Finite rational weights). *Let $\Gamma=\mathbb Z^k$, and let $\Lambda_1,\ldots,\Lambda_s$ be subgroups of $\Gamma$. Suppose that a finitely supported function $\nu:\Gamma\to\mathbb Q$ has zero pushforward on each $\Gamma/\Lambda_i$, and satisfies $$\sum_\gamma\nu(\gamma)=0,
 \qquad \sum_\gamma\nu(\gamma)\gamma=0,
 \qquad \sum_\gamma\nu(\gamma)\gamma\gamma^{\mathsf T}=C.$$ Let $U$ be a real $d\times k$ matrix with $UCU^{\mathsf T}=I_d$, where $d\geq1$. For every $\eta>0$, there are an integer $n\geq1$, a finitely supported $\mu:\Gamma\to\mathbb Q$, and $\zeta\in\mathbb Q_{>0}$ such that $\mu$ has zero pushforward on each $\Gamma/\Lambda_i$ and $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_\lambda\mu(\lambda)
       \frac{\lambda}{n}\frac{\lambda^{\mathsf T}}{n}
   &=\zeta C, \label{pairs:exact-moment}\\
 \sum_\lambda\mu(\lambda)_-
       \left\|U\frac{\lambda}{n}\right\|^2
   &<\eta\zeta. \label{pairs:small-negative}
\end{aligned}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pairs-rational"><summary>Proof of stmt-pairs-rational</summary>

<a id="pairs:convolution-moments"></a>
*Proof.* Take $b$ from Lemma <a href="#pairs:bump" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pairs:bump">8</a>. For each positive integer $n$, set $$d_n(\lambda)=n^{2-k}b(\lambda/n),
 \qquad
 \mu_n(\lambda)=(\nu*d_n)(\lambda)
      =\sum_\gamma\nu(\gamma)d_n(\lambda-\gamma).$$ These functions have finite support. Quotient pushforward commutes with convolution, so every required pushforward of $\mu_n$ is zero. More generally, for any finitely supported real function $d$ on $\Gamma$, the moment identities for $\nu$ give $$\label{pairs:convolution-moments}
 \sum_\lambda(\nu*d)(\lambda)=0,
 \qquad
 \sum_\lambda(\nu*d)(\lambda)\lambda\lambda^{\mathsf T}
       =\left(\sum_\beta d(\beta)\right)C.$$ Indeed, substitute $\lambda=\beta+\gamma$ in the double sum. The terms in $\beta\beta^{\mathsf T}$, $\beta\gamma^{\mathsf T}$ and $\gamma\beta^{\mathsf T}$ vanish by the zeroth and first moments of $\nu$. Thus, with $$\zeta_n=n^{-2}\sum_\lambda d_n(\lambda)
        =n^{-k}\sum_\lambda b(\lambda/n),$$ the identity in <a href="#pairs:exact-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pairs:exact-moment">[pairs:exact-moment]</a> holds exactly for $(\mu_n,\zeta_n)$, and Riemann sums give $\zeta_n\to\int b>0$.
